  What I did

  - Read AGENTS.md, the three Memory files, the archive conventions, both month
    inventories, all six September summaries and both October summaries oldest
    to newest, then both October 3 records in full.
  - Wrote 2026-10-03.1.SUMMARIZED.md (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/Sum
    marized/2026-10-03.1.SUMMARIZED.md) for the Codex session (five turns, three
    commits) and 2026-10-03.2.SUMMARIZED.md (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/202
    6/10/Summarized/2026-10-03.2.SUMMARIZED.md) for the parallel docs session
    (seven turns, seven commits). Every commit was checked against git for
    subject and changed files. The soft-reset commit from the docs session
    exists only as an unreferenced object, which the summary says.
  - SlopCop skips the archive, so I checked the links and turn anchors with a
    throwaway script, proved it fails on a bad link, and ran it over the four
    changed files. The October inventory
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/README.md) lists both summaries and
    today's record
    (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md).

  Prioritization, my recommendation

  The order below weighs how fresh your verdict is, how well-defined the work
  is, and whether it needs your eye or can run on its own.

  1. The Local Map on the hard cases. Your October 5 verdict is in, and the one
     criticism left standing is named: cycles and long connections still take
     exterior right-angle routes, and the Membrane Map's arrangement rules for
     many-hop pictures are "not fully migrated". The test is your own rule from
     October 1: symbols pinned on two or more files, then successive hops, on
     this repository and the estate, by eye and on the deck. This also settles
     whether the Membrane Map's hop-depth defect gets fixed or retired with the
     view. Needs you present, picture by picture.
  2. The scoped JSON export button. Your chosen shape for headless parity.
     Small, no layout risk, and the natural home for trimming what gets shared.
  3. Readable links. Proposed on October 3 and not yet agreed. The Pages
     workflow means colleagues' links matter today. Write readable parameters,
     keep reading old ?s= links, and decide whether the camera belongs in a
     link. Needs your yes on the constraint.
  4. A tree-sitter TypeScript adapter measured against scip-typescript. Engine
     work with an oracle and few forks. It is the September 27 fork answer
     applied to the last compiler-API language, and it cuts about 9 MB from any
     future scanner-in-file. The best candidate for autonomous or overnight
     work.
  5. reachable, vision step 4, which you wished for on September 26. Engine and
     CLI, independent of the design debates. One fork: what counts as an entry
     point per language.
  6. Directory pseudo-nodes, the directory-entered Local Map and the hover
     preview, as the next design probe after item 1. Three hover variants to
     playtest. Needs your taste.
  7. The self-saving single file, viewer first with a blank one-node board,
     under the rule that a file never writes uninvited. Builds on items 2 and 3.
  8. The Local Map to World Map transition, multiple scans composed at the World
     Map, and this repository's board as an estate. Design-first and large; you
     said much remains unanswered.
  9. Housekeeping when convenient: the Circuit Board's retirement on your word,
     the un-reproduced all-dim Force Graph report, Node 26 after October 28,
     oracles for C, Ruby and PowerShell, the Membrane Map's light theme and
     phone width.

  If you want something to start now while you are here, item 1 is where the
  design is. If you want something to run while you are away, item 4.

❯ Hmmm, so here's what I know about the current frontier models: Claude-family

  better in visual/computer-use work. While I feel like items 1 and 6 are my

  you can push forward and properly evaluate, I'm happy to work on it with you.
  Otherwise, I can leave that for GPT-6 Astra.

      125 +

          +a pin-visible reference, and draws every reference among them.
      127 +- Ranks files into columns by Tarjan's strongly connected comp
          +onents and longest path to a sink, so a provider sits as near 
          +its consumers as its chains allow; files in a cycle share a co
          +lumn.

          + are top-aligned in a CSS grid; the directory bands come from 
          +the Membrane's own band computation, with a scan-root shell ad
          +ded.

          +other wire, a skipped rank or a cycle, takes an orthogonal det
          +our: out to the right of its provider's column, up to a lane i
          +n a headroom above the whole picture, across, and down to the 
          +left of its consumer's column, each such wire in its own lane 
          +9 px apart.

          +n are hidden behind "+N symbols" and muted.

          +re the pinned ones and their one-hop neighbours; columns are h

          + themselves by Kahn's algorithm; 320 px fixed columns under la
          +bels; directory bands nested inside ancestor membranes from th
          +e least common ancestor outward, each label a link; within a g
          +roup, alphabetical; a reference whose provider pin is left of 
          +its consumer pin is a straight curve across whatever lies betw
          +een, and one that is not is a pair of French Corset stubs with
          + no path; connected but unpinned rows and pins are marked; hov
          +er dims everything not involving the hovered symbol.

● Item 1 is further along than the records suggested in one way and further
  behind in another. The native Local Map now has the right skeleton. What it
  lacks is the layout math on top of it, and that is pure code with measurable
  output.

  What I did

  I read both views' layout code in full, built both bundles, and put the
  shipped Local Map and the Membrane Map into the same state by URL on three
  scopes: the deck's five files around graph.ts, its four-file chain, and the
  estate's five files. I took the still-picture deck's measures on all six
  pictures and looked at each. The pictures, the scoreboard and a README naming
  each are in Screenshots/2026-10-05
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/2026-10-05/README.md), committed as 9859e454,
  so you can check what I saw.

  What the Local Map does today, after Codex's pass

  - Retains every pinned file and every endpoint of a pin-visible reference, and
    draws every reference among them. Nothing is hidden.
  - Ranks files into columns by strongly connected components and longest path
    to a sink, so a provider sits near its consumers. That is the placement fix
    you liked.
  - Within a column, files are in alphabetical order. Cards are top-aligned in a
    grid. Directory bands are the Membrane's own band code.
  - Any wire that skips a column or closes a cycle takes an orthogonal detour
    over the top of the whole picture, each in its own lane. That is the
    "hopping over a column" you dislike.

  What the pictures show, this repository's five files

  ┌───────────┬─────────┬───────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬─────────┐
  │           │ Legible │       │ Over the │ Crossing │ Backward │ Folders │
  │   View    │  facts  │ Wires │  top or  │  points  │   wires  │         │
  │           │         │       │  hidden  │          │          │ legible │
  ├───────────┼─────────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼─────────┤
  │ Local     │         │       │ 162      │          │          │         │
  │ Map,      │ 0 of 15 │   204 │ detours  │   14,112 │        0 │ 2 of 2  │
  │ retained  │         │       │          │          │          │         │
  ├───────────┼─────────┼───────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼─────────┤
  │ Membrane  │         │       │          │          │          │         │
  │ Map, all  │ 2 of 15 │   209 │ 55 stubs │      275 │      110 │ 7 of 7  │
  │ pinned    │         │       │          │          │          │         │
  └───────────┴─────────┴───────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴─────────┘

  The Local Map picture is a wall of vertical trunks in every gutter, with the
  camera framing only the subject's column. The Membrane picture is a calm grid
  with the folders named at a glance, bought by drawing half its wires backward,
  hiding fifty-five as stubs and crossing three cards. The chain and the estate
  rows agree in kind. Neither picture is what you want.

  The gap, named as code

  1. Routing: replace the one-headroom detour with routes that stay in the
     gutters they pass, or cross a card where that reads better.
  2. Ordering within columns: alphabetical today, with no crossing reduction.
     This is the textbook layered-graph step that the Membrane also lacks.
  3. Vertical placement: cards aligned to the pins they connect to, which the
     classic three-column mode already does with its alignment guides and the
     branch mode dropped.
  4. The fit: it frames the subject's neighbourhood, not the retained scope,
     which is why zero facts are legible.

  All four are pure functions over the ranked graph the code already computes.
  They test with Vitest on geometry and with the deck on the shipped bundles.

  Can I push it and evaluate it properly?

  Yes on the code and the measurement, with one honest caveat. This turn shows I
  can build, drive both views into one state, measure them and read the
  pictures accurately. The risk with me is the October 1 one: Rings scored well
  and read badly, because I let numbers pick a visual rule. The remedy is
  procedural, not a different model: build inside the shipped Local Map, compare
  each change against the Membrane picture by picture with the deck as the
  floor, and stop at the three design forks for your eye. Those forks are what a
  cycle looks like (a local loop, stubs with a count, or lanes), whether a long
  reference may cross a card as the Membrane allows, and whether the fit may
  shrink below reading size to show the whole scope.

  My recommendation is that I take it, in that order, with you reviewing
  pictures at each fork. If you would rather Astra take it, the checkpoint in
  today's record
  (AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md#turn-2) and the
  screenshot README give it the same starting point.

● Sounds good, with one correction to the premise before I start.

  The Membrane's layout math is smaller than its picture suggests. I read all of
  it on this turn. Its columns are hop depth from the pinned files, which is
  the standing defect you confirmed on October 1. Within a column it is
  alphabetical, like the Local Map. It has no crossing reduction and no pin
  alignment. What makes its picture calm is four things: the nested ancestor
  labels, a fixed-width grid, long references drawn straight across whatever
  lies between, and backward references hidden as stubs. The bands already moved
  over on October 3. So this is a build of the standard layered-drawing steps
  that neither view has, with the Membrane picture as the reference and the
  Local Map's cards kept as they are.

  The plan, for your yes

  1. Ordering. A barycenter sweep over the ranked columns that orders the band
     rows and the files within each band by where their connected pins sit in
     the neighbouring columns. Ties stay alphabetical. Pure function with Vitest
     fixtures that contain crossings the sweep removes.
  2. Vertical placement. Each card's first connected pin aligns with its partner
     where the card above allows. Pure placement math applied as per-card
     offsets.
  3. Fit. Frame the whole retained scope when it fits at reading size. Otherwise
     keep the subject at scale 1 as now. Never below reading size, which your
     existing rules already say.
  4. Measurement. A retained-scope row added to the still-picture spec, the deck
     before and after on the three scopes, pictures into the dated screenshots
     folder, and Playwright regressions for the invariants: no wire over the
     top, no wire across a card it does not end at, every wire flowing.

  Two forks, yours to close

  - Skipped-rank references. My pick: thread them through the intermediate
    columns in slots reserved during placement, the way layered drawing uses
    dummy nodes. Never over the top, never across a card. The alternative is the
    Membrane's way, straight across cards.
  - Cycles. My pick: break each cycle at its feedback references so the rest
    ranks forward, and draw each back reference as a French Corset pair with a
    count on the card and the full route drawn on hover. The reduction says what
    it hides and leaves a way back. The alternative is a loop routed around the
    outside of the columns it spans.

  What I need from you is a yes on the plan and your answer on the two forks.
  Items 1 through 4 are the same under either answer, so say go and I will start
  there while you decide.

  may or may not be related to the current task.</ide_opened_file>
❯ >Ordering. A barycenter sweep over the ranked columns that orders the band
  rows and the files within each band by where their connected pins sit in the
  neighbouring columns. Ties stay alphabetical. Pure function with Vitest
  fixtures that contain crossings the sweep removes.

  Again sounds good, albeit I do wonder if the Vitest fixtures would be stale
  quickly once the layouts improve? How do we make such a test not become
  divergent from the implementation it's testing on that front? Am I maybe
  asking a dumb question and misunderstanding here?

  >Skipped-rank references. My pick: thread them through the intermediate
  columns in slots reserved during placement, the way layered drawing uses
  dummy nodes. Never over the top, never across a card. The alternative is the
  Membrane's way, straight across cards.

  Hmm... never say never eh? I mean truthfully, the whole point is just to make
  sure that we can maximize the legibility of a complex view. It's more
  legible if things have a directionality and don't have overlaps. I wonder if
  fixing the right angles might also potentially involve letting the cards have
  more vertical space to breathe to enable their bezier curves to not slam
  into nodes above/below them? I feel like there may be places where our layout
  math must choose the lesser of two evils, trying to balance competing
  forces. Generally that's where my mind goes to with solutions. Again, let me
  know if you disagree.

  >Cycles. My pick: break each cycle at its feedback references so the rest
  ranks forward, and draw each back reference as a French Corset pair with a
  count on the card and the full route drawn on hover. The reduction says what
  it hides and leaves a way back. The alternative is a loop routed around the
  outside of the columns it spans.

  I know at once point we actually created a duplicate node for the cycles, but
  that was when the duplicate node didn't need to potentially know all of its
  dependencies afterward. This was something that could be done in the Local
  Map only with a 3-column layout, allowing a file to be a dependent and a
  dependency relative to the selected node. You're right that it won't work
  here, where we really do need to look around. That said, I do feel like we
  should be open to the possibility that ugly design may produce ugly
  visualization _and_ complex design may _require_ 3D visualization on the
  force graph when things get too complex. We want to maximize the complexity
  we can cleanly present the user before the layout 'splodes, and perhaps when
  it is apparent that the layout ain't gonna handle this well, we kinda nudge
  the user to maybe switch to the 3D force graph view?

● Your three answers close both forks and reopen nothing, so I'll record them
  and start the pass. First the record and Memory, then the remaining files the
  ordering and placement work touches.

       6 +/** A gap in a column a wire passes through: the column's horiz
         +ontal extent, widened by a margin, at the lane's height. */    
       7 +export interface Passage { left: number; right: number; y: numb
         +er }                                                           
       8 +                                                               
       9 +/** One piece of a threaded route: a curve across a gutter, or 
         +a straight run along a lane through a column. */               
      10 +export type RoutePiece =                                       
      11 +  | { kind: "curve"; from: RoutePoint; to: RoutePoint }        
      12 +  | { kind: "lane"; from: RoutePoint; to: RoutePoint };        
      13 +                                                               
      14 +/**                                                            
      15 + * The curve the Local Map draws between two pins: a cubic whos
         +e control points                                               
      16 + * leave and arrive horizontally, so a wire departs an offering
         + pin to the right                                              
      17 + * and enters a using pin from the left; a near-vertical hop is
         + a quadratic.                                                  
      18 + * Returns the path commands after the move to `from`.         
      19 + */                                                            
      20 +export function curveTo(from: RoutePoint, to: RoutePoint, tunin
         +g: BezierTuning): string {                                     
      21 +  const horizontalDirection = to.x >= from.x ? 1 : -1;         
      22 +  const gapX = Math.abs(to.x - from.x);                        
      23 +  if (gapX < 24) {                                             
      24 +    const midY = (from.y + to.y) / 2;                          
      25 +    return `Q ${from.x} ${midY} ${to.x} ${to.y}`;              
      26 +  }                                                            
      27 +  const stubBase = Math.max(gapX * tuning.stubFactor, tuning.st
         +ubMin);                                                        
      28 +  const stubLimit = Math.max(44, gapX - tuning.stubMaxOffset); 
      29 +  const stub = Math.min(stubBase, stubLimit);                  
      30 +  const control1X = from.x + horizontalDirection * stub;       
      31 +  const control2X = to.x - horizontalDirection * stub;         
      32 +  const deltaY = to.y - from.y;                                
      33 +  const control1Y = from.y + deltaY * tuning.verticalOffset;   
      34 +  const control2Y = to.y - deltaY * tuning.verticalOffset;     
      35 +  return `C ${control1X} ${control1Y} ${control2X} ${control2Y}
         + ${to.x} ${to.y}`;                                             
      36  }

● Found three real bugs: the sweep recomputed columns from stale positions
  simultaneously, a three-cycle broke two references instead of one due to a
  scoring tie, and my "never worse" test compared against a metric that ignores
  threaded wires. I'll fix these by sweeping column by column, applying
  Eades-Lin-Smyth ordering for cycles, and ensuring comparisons use consistent
  metrics. · summary

● Update(AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md)
  ⎿  Added 11 lines
      254  - Carried into direction.md as a dated bullet in the owner's w
           ords.
      255  
      256  **Execution order, for resumption after compaction**: (A) cycl
           e breaking in the ranking, so every reference ranks forward ex
           cept a flagged feedback set; (B) ordering of band rows and fil
           es by a barycenter sweep with the threaded references as virtu
           al nodes; (C) placement: per-card vertical offsets aligning fi
           rst connected pins, and reserved gaps where threaded wires pas
           s; the router draws threaded wires through those gaps and back
            references as Corset pairs with counts and a hover route; (D)
            the fit; (E) the nudge from the layout's strain; (F) the reta
           ined-scope row in the still-picture spec, Playwright invariant

      260 +- `branches.ts`: `rankBranches` now returns `{ columns, back }
          +`. Cycles are broken by the Eades–Lin–Smyth provider-first ord
          +er within each strongly connected component; the references th
          +at read backward in that order are `back`; every other referen
          +ce ranks forward by longest path to a sink as before. `buildBr
          +anches` computes each card's visible rows to give every wire a
          + height on its card, and runs the ordering; `BranchGraph` gain
          +s `back` and `order`. `edgeKey` is exported.                  
      261 +- `branch-order.ts` (new): `orderBranches` builds a virtual no
          +de for every column a reference skips, takes the Membrane's ba
          +nds, and sweeps column by column against the column just settl
          +ed, left then right, repacking the band rows once per pass fro
          +m where every member's wires lead; the fewest-crossing order i
          +s kept. Output: the reordered bands, the columns top to bottom
          +, the lanes (a gap after a named file, or above the column's f
          +irst file) and each threaded reference's passage through each 
          +lane.                                                         
      262 +- `branch-routing.ts`: the over-the-top detour is gone. `curve
          +To` is the native curve as a pure function (the DOM path build
          +er now calls it) and `threadedRoute` composes curve, lane, cur
          +ve, lane, curve. Lane constants: 7 px per wire, 6 px padding, 
          +10 px margin outside a column.

          +al-pass-through` spacer sized to its lane above the column's f
          +irst file or after the named file, and appends "N references r

          +hrough the measured spacers; back references draw as Corset st

      101 +  /** How many references the drawn exploration threads throug
          +h lanes, and how many it draws as stubs. */                   
      102 +  getStrain(): BranchStrain | null {                          
      103 +    const branches = this.branches;                           
      104 +    if (!branches) return null;                               
      105 +    const columnOf = new Map(branches.columns.flatMap((nodes, 
          +column) => nodes.map(node => [node.id, column] as const)));   
      106 +    let threaded = 0;                                         
      107 +    for (const edge of branches.subgraph.links) {             
      108 +      if (edge.sourceId === edge.targetId || branches.back.has
          +(edgeKey(edge))) continue;                                    
      109 +      const a = columnOf.get(edge.targetId), b = columnOf.get(
          +edge.sourceId);                                               
      110 +      if (a !== undefined && b !== undefined && b > a + 1) thr
          +eaded++;                                                      
      111 +    }                                                         
      112 +    return { threaded, back: branches.back.size, columns: bran
          +ches.columns.length };                                        
      113 +  }

       9  ## Authored
      10  ### Purpose
      11 -_Pending authored purpose_                                     
      11  
      12 +Orders a ranked Local Map exploration so that its wires cross a
         +s little as the directory bands allow, and reserves a lane thro
         +ugh every column that a reference skips.                       
      13 +                                                               
      14  ### Notes
      14 -_Pending notes_                                                
      15  
      16 +This is the ordering and lane step of layered graph drawing, wi
         +th the Membrane Map's directory bands as a constraint: files of
         + one directory stay together in a column, and a band's row is t
         +he same in every column it spans. The sweep walks the columns l
         +eft to right and then right to left, settling each column by th
         +e barycenter of what it is wired to in the column just settled;
         + the band rows are repacked once per pass from where every memb
         +er's wires lead; the order with the fewest crossings between ad
         +jacent columns is kept. A reference that skips columns gets a v
         +irtual node in each column it passes, slotted among that column
         +'s files by the same barycenter, so that the gap it names becom
         +es the lane the renderer leaves between two cards and the route
         +r threads the wire through. Nothing is hidden and nothing is bu
         +ndled: every reference keeps its own wire and its own lane plac
         +e.                                                             
      17 +                                                               
      18 +The best order is a snapshot with its own arrays: a pass settle
         +s columns in place, and a snapshot that shared them drifted fro
         +m its bands, which drew the page in one order and the lanes in 
         +another until the estate's five-file picture showed sixteen wir
         +es across cards ([the October 5 session](../../../../../../../.
         +./AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md
         +#turn-4)). The test that the bands walk to exactly the returned
         + columns keeps that invariant. Written on 2026-10-05 under the 
         +owner's answers on routing as a balance rather than a rule.

         +vider-first order of Eades, Lin and Smyth, the references that 
         +read backward in that order are returned as `back`, and everyth
         +ing else ranks forward; the renderer draws a back reference as 
         +French Corset stubs with its route on hover, under the owner's 
         +words of 2026-10-05 that ugly design may produce ugly visualiza
         +tion and the picture must say what it hides. The ranking then h
         +ands its columns to `branch-order.ts`, which orders them and re
         +serves the lanes.

● Update(.mdmd/layer-3/architectural-decisions.mdmd.md)
  ⎿  Added 9 lines, removed 1 line
      170  - **What landed** (`8fd76252`, `61c297f2`, `9969abe1`, `6b881f
           00`): the Force Graph centres a requested file without resetti
           ng the simulation or the viewing direction, and yields to manu
           al navigation; the sample programs keep their roles after the 
           fixture move (148 implementation files and 12 assets had becom
           e tests) and the oracle's persisted file references are relati
           ve to their own file, with all 21 comparison reports unchanged
           ; the Local Map keeps every pinned file's neighbourhood with e
           very reference among them, cycles sharing a column and skipped
            ranks routed outside, and the old truncating multi-hop render
           er is retired; the Membrane Map's directory bands are drawn be
           hind the Local Map's cards; hover isolates transiently even un
           der pins; a whole-file pin includes every symbol and a symbol 
           pin survives beneath it; the Force Graph draws one counted lin
           e per file pair over the canonical references; and a deliberat
           e zoom joins the two perspectives, with a wheel boundary that 
           needs a second nudge (the owner's "speedbump"), cards folding 
           into named points that take their force positions and unfoldin
           g on the way back.
      171  - **Rules set on the way**: the thing under the eye stays put 
           while the world rearranges around it, at a transition relative
            to the last clicked node; a change of perspective (Local Map 
           to Force Graph) is not a change of scale; no directory membran
           es around force clusters, because "a directory could materiali
           ze in many clusters!"; pin count alone is no trigger for depth
           ; the path search's default bound is the graph's simple-path l
           ength, not ten hops ([Turn 13](../../AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHi
           story/2026/10/2026-10-02.1.record.md#turn-13)).
      172  - **Verdict**: "The reorientation of the nodes into place is p
           erfect" ([Turn 14](../../AI-Agent-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/1
           0/2026-10-02.1.record.md#turn-14)). Deferred by the owner in t
           he same words: the straight file wire splitting into its symbo
           l curves during the zoom. Still disliked: the Local Map's exte
           rior right-angle routes that hop over intervening content.
      173 -- **October 3 follow-through**: the owner replaced the layered
          --pin rule with click-retains and close-prunes: a card click re
          -tains all interfaces, a symbol click toggles one effective row
          -, and X releases that file's pins while keeping a compact neig
          -hbor if other pins need it. The implementation preserves the a

      177 +- **Decision**: the retained exploration is laid out by the st
          +eps of layered graph drawing, inside the shipped Local Map, wi
          +th the Membrane Map's picture as the reference and its directo
          +ry bands as a constraint. Ranking stays provider-first by long
          +est forward chain; each cycle is broken at the references that
          + read backward in the provider-first order of Eades, Lin and S
          +myth; the band rows and the files within them are ordered by a
          + barycenter sweep that walks the columns both ways and keeps t
          +he fewest crossings; a reference that skips columns gets a vir
          +tual node in each column it passes, which becomes a lane the r
          +enderer leaves between two cards and the router threads the wi
          +re through; a back reference is drawn as French Corset stubs a
          +t its pins, counted on the card, with its full route shown whi
          +le a symbol's hover highlights it. Nothing is hidden and nothi
          +ng is bundled.                                                
      178 +- **Why**: on 2026-10-05 the owner asked where this work stood
          +. Both views were put into one state on three scopes and measu
          +red ([Screenshots/2026-10-05](../../AI-Agent-Workspace/Screens
          +hots/2026-10-05/README.md)): on this repository's five-file sc
          +ope the Local Map drew 162 of 204 wires as orthogonal detours 
          +over the top of the picture, with 14,112 crossing points, whil
          +e the Membrane Map drew 110 of 209 backward and hid 55 as stub
          +s. The Membrane's layout math turned out smaller than its pict
          +ure: hop-depth columns, alphabetical order, long references st
          +raight across cards. So this is a build of the ordering and la
          +ne steps neither view had, not a port ([Turn 3](../../AI-Agent
          +-Workspace/ChatHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md#turn-3))
          +.                                                             
      179 +- **The owner's rules** ([Turn 4](../../AI-Agent-Workspace/Cha
          +tHistory/2026/10/2026-10-05.1.record.md#turn-4)): routing is a
          + balance, not a rule, "never say never"; the aim is legibility
          +, directionality and no overlaps, with "more vertical space to
          + breathe" for the curves, which is what the lanes are; a cycle
          + may look ugly, since "ugly design may produce ugly visualizat
          +ion"; and when the layout strains, nudge toward the Force Grap
          +h. The nudge is driven by the layout's own strain, the threade
          +d and back references counted, against a threshold in Tuning, 
          +never by the number of pins.

      82  
      83  ## Where this stands
      84  
      85 -_As of 2026-10-03._                                            
      85 +_As of 2026-10-05._                                            
      86  
      87  Works today: the single-folder map for TypeScript and JavaScrip
          t through the compiler API, and for C#, Python, Java, Go and Ru
          st through tree-sitter, each measured against its compiler's ow
          n resolution; hand-written scanners for C, Ruby, PowerShell, SQ
          L, ASP.NET markup, .NET configuration, HTML, CSS and JSON, with
           no oracle yet for C or Ruby; manifests (`.csproj`, `packages.c
          onfig`, `package.json`) as Live Docs; one grammar that renders 
          and parses every Live Doc, proved by a round trip over the whol
          e corpus; deterministic regeneration with authored sections pre
          served; one graph index derived from the docs, which `inspect`,
           lint, the oracle and the Explorer all read; and the board text
          , one file of things and connections that lint checks and the C
          LI joins to the docs. The static Explorer draws five views over
           that graph: the World Map, the landing view when the bundle ca
          rries a board; the Local Map, which keeps every pinned file's n
          eighbourhood with every reference among them, draws their direc
          tories as bands, and finds a path only in the map's reading dir
          ection; the Force Graph, which centres a file on request and dr
          aws one counted line per file pair; the Membrane Map, which a t
          hing on the World Map opens into; and the Circuit Board. A deli
          berate zoom joins the Local Map and the Force Graph: cards fold
           into named points that take their force positions, and unfold 
          again on the way back. Two instruments keep the pictures honest
          : the design audit fails a test when words collide or are cut o
          ff, and the still-picture deck measures what one picture shows 
          legibly.

      177  - **Decision**: the retained exploration is laid out by the st
           eps of layered graph drawing, inside the shipped Local Map, wi

● Update(packages/engine/src/tooling/githubSlugger.ts)
  ⎿  Added 23 lines
       97    return new GitHubSlugger();
       98  }
       99  
      100 +/**                                                           
      101 + * The text of a heading as GitHub slugs it: the rendered text
          +, not the markdown.                                           
      102 + *                                                            
      103 + * GitHub builds a heading's anchor from its rendered content,
          + so a link's text                                             
      104 + * stands without its address, an image without its source, in
          +line code without                                             
      105 + * its backticks, and emphasis without its delimiters. Asteris
          +ks and backticks                                              
      106 + * the slug pattern removes anyway; underscores it keeps, sinc
          +e `snake_case`                                                
      107 + * belongs in a slug, so an underscore is dropped only where i
          +t delimits                                                    
      108 + * emphasis: at the start of a word or at its end. Without thi
          +s, a heading such                                             
      109 + * as `### Decision _(Recorded 2026-10-05)_` slugs to a form G
          +itHub never makes.                                            
      110 + *                                                            
      111 + * @param markdown - The heading's text as written, after the 
          +ATX hashes.                                                   
      112 + * @returns The text to pass to {@link slug}.                 
      113 + */                                                           
      114 +export function slugText(markdown: string): string {          
      115 +  return markdown                                             
      116 +    .replace(/!?\[([^\]]*)\]\([^)]*\)/g, "$1")                
      117 +    .replace(/\[([^\]]*)\]\[[^\]]*\]/g, "$1")                 
      118 +    .replace(/`+/g, "")                                       
      119 +    .replace(/(^|[^\p{L}\p{N}])_+(?=\S)/gu, "$1")             
      120 +    .replace(/(?<=\S)_+(?=$|[^\p{L}\p{N}])/gu, "");           
      121 +}                                                             
      122 +                                                              
      123  function createOccurrences(): Record<string, number> {
      124    return Object.create(null) as Record<string, number>;
      125  }

● I measured before answering, so two of the three have causes rather than
  impressions. Nothing in the code changed; the record and your re-exported
  capture are committed as 7ab7d232.

  The guitar strings

  You are half right about the codebase's shape. A symbol used by fifteen files
  is fifteen references, and both views draw all of them. Giving each its own
  string through every gap is the drawing's choice, though, not the code's. The
  layered-drawing answer is to bundle. References leaving one offering pin share
  one lane slot through every column they pass together, and a member leaves
  the bundle only at the gutter before its consumer's column, which is where the
  eye needs the separation. Pupyrev, Nachmanson and Kaufmann did this for
  layered drawings in 2010 by merging the dummy nodes of edges with a common
  source. In the estate's five-file picture, the twenty strings above
  IPaymentHub come from two pins and would become two strokes. The fifteen below
  IPaymentService would become one. Each reference keeps its own path, so
  hovering still traces one reference, and the shared run can thicken with its
  member count so the picture does not hide what it carries. The deck's crossing
  counter already ignores contacts shallower than fifteen degrees, so
  coincident strokes would not read as crossings. Its shared-channel count would
  rise, by design. I would do this in the ordering rather than only in the
  drawing, so lanes are sized by bundles and the sweep stops arranging fifteen
  identical strings.

  Wires through Portal/Services

  Those two wires pass through that box because the ordering places a lane after
  a file, and the only file in that column is GatewayClient, inside
  Portal/Services. The truthful fix is placement, not glass. A lane should
  belong to the deepest directory in its column that holds one of the wire's
  ends, here Portal, so the wire runs in Portal's box beside the Services box
  instead of through it. Glass is possible. The overlay is one layer, so it
  would mean per-wire masks cut from the band boxes, with the foreign piece
  blurred and dimmed. But it would dress a wrong placement as intended. What
  remains after the placement fix is curves in gutters that cross a directory
  spanning that gutter. I would add a deck measure for samples inside a foreign
  directory, for both views, and decide on glass with that number and picture in
  front of us.

  The overflow

  Measured in the built page:

  ┌──────────────────────┬────────┐
  │                      │ Width  │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ GatewayClient's card │ 256 px │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Its column           │ 244 px │
  └──────────────────────┴────────┘

  The card's width is 100% of its container plus its own padding and border, so
  it is always 26 px wider than the room its content asked the grid for.
  Border-box sizing ends it in the built page. The subgrid is innocent, since
  removing the bands' padding shrinks the tracks by exactly that padding. One
  more defect seen while measuring: the whole layout sits in a box labelled "/"
  with its own padding, from the native-view pass. The Membrane draws no box at
  the root. I would remove it.

  1. Border-box cards and no root box, with pictures of both Local Map modes and
     the Circuit Board, which shares the card.

     foreign-directory measure, and a Playwright rule that no straight run lies
     in a foreign directory.

     consumer's gutter, stroke width by member count.

● Your reading of the repository picture is right, and the fix is a known step I
  have not built yet.

  What those strings are

  The lanes above graph.ts hold one slot per offering pin of document.ts. That
  file offers eighteen symbols, and each has consumers beyond graph.ts, so
  eighteen cables pass. Each is a real bundle. The crossings you see come from
  the card, not the lanes. A card lists its rows in the Live Doc's symbol order,
  so the rows whose cables go up to the lane are scattered among the rows wired
  straight to graph.ts, board.ts and boardGraph.ts, and every cable from a
  lower row climbs across the wires above it.

  The step

  Layered drawing orders ports after it orders nodes. Here that means sorting
  each card's rows by where their wires go: a row's key is the mean height of
  its wires' far ends, offers to the right and uses to the left, and the rows
  sort by it. Rows whose cables leave for the lane above rise to the top, rows
  wired to the neighbour card sit level with it, rows wired downward sink. Then
  one more column sweep, so the crossing count sees the new heights. The classic
  no-pins view keeps the Live Doc's order. Internals stays last either way.

  The fork

  - Order by wires (my recommendation). The card reads like a pinout. The cost
    is that the same file's rows stand in a different order in different
    explorations. Names are on the rows, so a symbol is found by name either
    way.
  - Partition only. Rows whose wires pass on go to the top, the rest keep the
    Live Doc's order. This is what you described literally. It fixes this
    picture and leaves crossings the first option removes, such as two
    neighbour-bound rows wired to cards above and below each other.

  Tests would be properties: a row whose wires all lead above another's stands
  above it, crossings never worse than before the row step, same order every
  run. Say which, and I'll build it with pictures of the same three scopes.

● Written for: the owner, who named the objective and asked me to continue.

  You have named the step I have not built. Layered drawing has four: rank,
  order, order the ports, place. The first three are in. Cards still stand where
  the grid stacks them, so graph.ts sits level with document.ts's top rows
  whether or not its wires want that. Lowering it is what a placement step would
  do on its own, if it serves the total.

  The objective, measured first

  I would add your reward to the deck before touching the layout: the total
  drawn length of the wires in a frame, and the mean per wire. Then the step is
  judged by your number, not by crossings.

  The step

  Every item in a column, cards and lanes alike, gets a vertical position. A
  card wants to sit where its wired pins meet their partners, the median of the
  partners' heights less the pin's own offset on the card. A lane wants the
  median of its bundles' ends. The constraints are the order the sweep chose, a
  gap between neighbours, and directories that do not interleave, so a directory
  above another stays above it in every column they share. The solver sweeps
  the columns left to right and back, placing each column as close to its wishes
  as the constraints allow, and keeps the placement with the shortest total.

  What has to change underneath

  The picture is a CSS grid whose rows are shared across columns. Pushing
  graph.ts down in its row pushes every column's content in that row down with
  it, so the grid cannot express the step. Cards and lanes would be positioned
  from the solver instead, with the directory boxes drawn from their members'
  extents, nested with padding. The same elements keep their names, so the
  perspective transition, the router and the deck read the page as they do
  today. Heights come from a measuring render before placement, as the lanes are
  measured back now.

  The forks

  - Placement inside the grid, by spacers above cards. Cheap, and it would nudge
    graph.ts, board.ts and boardGraph.ts down. But a spacer in one column moves
    every column sharing the row, so the result is coupled and partly blind. I
    do not recommend it.
  - Free placement, as above. My recommendation. It is the step the literature
    has, it serves your total directly, and it removes the card-versus-pixel
    mismatch that cost the chain scopes tonight. The cost is a renderer
    restructure and a full day's pass with pictures.

  One more choice I would make myself unless you object: the solver's own
  measure is vertical displacement between pin and partner, since the columns
  fix the horizontal spans. The deck measures the true drawn length.

  Say go on the free placement and I'll build it, measure it on all four scopes,
  and bring the pictures.

❯ Can I ask about the right step forward pedagogically?

  What is the durable, corect, permanent implementation? Implement that.

  The Pythagorean theorem forgot to go obsolete. Seems that when you're correct
  100%, you're correct forever.

● The durable form is the exact one: the placement that minimizes your total
  subject to the constraints, not a heuristic that approximates it. That is the
  coordinate step of Gansner, Koutsofios, North and Vo, solved by network
  simplex, the method Graphviz has run for thirty years. I'll build that, with
  cards placed from the solution rather than by the grid. First I need to see
  what the perspective transition reads from the page, since the page's
  structure changes.

● Written for: the owner, who asked for the durable, correct, permanent
  implementation of the placement step.

  Built and committed as b5cc8536, 31 files, after a full gate: 969 unit tests,
  50 integration tests, 623 Live Docs with no drift, both audits clean, 90
  Playwright tests.

  What the durable implementation is

  The placement of cards on the vertical axis is a linear program. Its
  constraints are differences: a card below another by at least its height and
  the gap, a box's edge above its members by the label's room, a sibling box
  below another by the band gap. Its objective is your reward, the weighted sum
  of vertical distances between the two pins of every wire, with a bundle's
  shared run weighing its members. Gansner, Koutsofios, North and Vo solved
  exactly this in 1993 by the network simplex method, and Graphviz has run it
  since. That is what the Local Map runs now. The solver is tested against
  enumeration on forty small instances, so its optimum is the optimum. Boxes are
  drawn tight beneath the wires by a million-to-one weight, so no wire is ever
  traded for a box.

  What you will see

  The fourth section of the day's screenshots (AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/20
  26-10-05/README.md#placed-by-the-exact-step-the-same-night):

  - Five files, placed (AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/2026-10-05/repository-fiv
    e-local-map-placed.png): graph.ts dropped to meet document.ts's rows,
    boardGraph.ts with it. The move you described is what the optimum chose.
  - Estate chain, placed (AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/2026-10-05/estate-chain
    -local-map-placed.png): Models and Controllers level with the wires between
    them, every box tight around its cards.
  - Estate five, placed (AI-Agent-Workspace/Screenshots/2026-10-05/estate-five-l
    ocal-map-placed.png): PaymentResult's cable runs level into its lane.

  Your reward, measured

  The deck now measures the total drawn wire length. The previous commit, built
  beside this one:

  ┌─────────────┬──────────┬─────────┬─────────┬───────┬──────────┬───────┐
  │             │  Total   │         │ Picture │       │ Crossing │       │
  │    Scope    │  wire    │  after  │  height │ after │  spots   │ after │
  │             │ length   │         │  before │       │  before  │       │
  │             │  before  │         │         │       │          │       │
  ├─────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ Repository, │ 681,249  │ 610,394 │  6,236  │ 5,653 │      438 │   636 │
  │  five files │       px │      px │      px │    px │          │       │
  ├─────────────┼──────────┼─────────┼─────────┼───────┼──────────┼───────┤
  │ Repository, │ 793,840  │ 600,503 │  6,460  │ 5,379 │      736 │ 1,223 │
  │  chain      │       px │      px │      px │    px │          │       │

  └─────────────┴──────────┴─────────┴─────────┴───────┴──────────┴───────┘

  The length fell on every scope, by a tenth to two fifths, and every picture is
  shorter. The crossings rose. Cards packed to their partners cross more of the
  wires that pass them, and this step minimizes length, not crossings. Whether
  that is the right price is yours to say with the pictures open.

  What broke on the way, and was fixed

  Three things, each caught by a probe or a picture before the gate: directory
  labels wrapped to one character per line in boxes not yet given a width, which
  put 250 px of slack above every card; a card's row of test names wrapped at
  the column width but not at its own, which grew it a line after measurement;
  and a variable named for the column wrapper shadowed the column number, which
  lost half the lanes. The branch, perspective and deck tests hold all three
  now.

  Left open

  The glass question for wires crossing a directory that holds neither of their
  ends, with the deck's number in front of you. And the solver recomputes its
  cut values after every exchange, milliseconds today; a scope of a thousand
  wires would want the incremental update the paper describes.
